\begin{lemma}[Complex sums and changing phase weights]
  \label{L:interval-complex}
  Let $A_0$ be bounded, nonnegative and selfadjoint, and let $V$ be bounded on
  the same complex Hilbert space. Write $A_\rho = \rho^2+A_0$. The complex phase
  conclusion of Lemma~\ref{L:complex-phase} holds for $L = A_0-V$ whenever $V$
  is bounded, not necessarily selfadjoint,
  \[
    \|A_\rho^{-1/2}VA_\rho^{-1/2}\|\le D\rho^{a-2},\qquad \rho>0,
    \quad 0<a<1,\quad D\le\delta_a\Lambda^{2-a}.
  \]
  Its bound is $100e^{2\Lambda h}$ in $X_\Lambda$. If a piecewise-constant
  potential is the sum of connected-interval fields, assign a positive weight
  $\omega_i$ to each field present and put $\Lambda(t) =
  \lambda+\sum_i\omega_i\mathbf1_{I_i}(t)$. The total loss from changing between
  these phase norms, starting and ending in $X_\lambda$, is at most
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-metric}
    \prod_i(1+\omega_i/\lambda).
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We repeat the resolvent and numerical-range estimates for a general bounded
  complex potential, retaining adjoint comparisons. We then count the
  phase-norm losses at jumps of the active interval set.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We obtain the four ordered root comparisons
  without selfadjointness.
  The adjoint has the identical sandwich bound, since
  \[
    A_\rho^{-1/2}V^*A_\rho^{-1/2}
    =(A_\rho^{-1/2}VA_\rho^{-1/2})^*.
  \]
  The form estimate at $\rho = \Lambda$ gives
  $\operatorname{Re}\langle(L+\Lambda^2)u,u\rangle \ge (1-\delta_a)\langle
  A_\Lambda u,u\rangle$. The shifted operator and its adjoint thus have spectra
  in the right half-plane, where their principal inverse roots are defined. Put
  $K_\kappa = A_\kappa^{-1/2}VA_\kappa^{-1/2}$ and $\kappa =
  (\Lambda^2+t)^{1/2}$. The resolvent difference is exactly
  \[
    (L+\Lambda^2+t)^{-1}-A_\kappa^{-1}
    =A_\kappa^{-1/2}(1-K_\kappa)^{-1}K_\kappa A_\kappa^{-1/2}.
  \]
  Its product on the left with $A_\Lambda^{1/2}$ has norm at most
  $D\kappa^{a-3}/(1-\delta_a)$. The inverse-root integral in
  Lemma~\ref{L:complex-phase} therefore gives the same $1/6$ bound. More
  explicitly, put $L_\Lambda = L+\Lambda^2$ and $Q =
  A_\Lambda^{1/2}L_\Lambda^{-1/2}$. Then $\|Q-I\| \le 1/6$, so $\|Q\| \le 7/6$
  and $\|Q^{-1}\| \le 6/5$ by its Neumann series. Taking adjoints under the
  norm-convergent resolvent integral gives $(L_\Lambda^{-1/2})^* =
  (L_\Lambda^*)^{-1/2}$. Apply the same estimate to $L^* = A_0-V^*$ and take
  adjoints. This bounds $L_\Lambda^{-1/2}A_\Lambda^{1/2}$ by $7/6$ and its
  inverse $A_\Lambda^{-1/2}L_\Lambda^{1/2}$ by $6/5$. Together with $Q$ and
  $Q^{-1} = L_\Lambda^{1/2}A_\Lambda^{-1/2}$, these are all four ordered
  comparisons in~\eqref{E:phase-root-comparisons}, each at most two.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We retain the numerical-range and phase-block
  bounds.
  For a unit vector $u$ put $s = \|A_0^{1/2}u\|$ and $\langle Lu,u\rangle =
  x+iy$. The form estimate at $\rho = \Lambda$ and, separately, at $\rho = s$
  gives
  \[
    |\langle Vu,u\rangle|\le D\Lambda^{a-2}(s^2+\Lambda^2),
    \qquad |\langle Vu,u\rangle|\le2Ds^a.
  \]
  At $s = 0$ the second bound follows by $\rho\downarrow0$. Thus, $x+\Lambda^2
  \ge (1-\delta_a)(s^2+\Lambda^2)$ and $|y| \le 2\delta_a\Lambda^{2-a}s^a$.
  These are the two inequalities used for the numerical-range parabola in
  Lemma~\ref{L:complex-phase}. Its scalar bounds and the bounded-operator
  Crouzeix--Palencia theorem apply to $L$ without selfadjointness. The four
  weighted block formulas there use only functions of $L$ and the root
  comparisons just proved. Their norms are at most $3e^{\Lambda h}$,
  $12(1+\Lambda h)e^{\Lambda h}$, $12e^{\Lambda h}$ and $12e^{\Lambda h}$. Their
  sum is at most $100e^{2\Lambda h}$. This uses neither selfadjointness of $V$
  nor commutation of $A_\Lambda$ with $L$.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We charge each norm loss only when an interval
  ends.
  For a common phase vector,
  \[
    \|z\|_{\rho_{\rm new}}
    \le\max\{1,\rho_{\rm old}/\rho_{\rm new}\}\|z\|_{\rho_{\rm old}}.
  \]
  At a jump, write $E$ for ending fields, $S$ for starting fields, and $R$ for
  those remaining. The ratio is at most
  \[
    \frac{\lambda+\sum_R\omega_i+\sum_E\omega_i}
    {\lambda+\sum_R\omega_i+\sum_S\omega_i}
    \le1+\sum_E\omega_i/\lambda
    \le\prod_E(1+\omega_i/\lambda).
  \]
  Starting intervals enlarge the denominator of the old-to-new weight ratio
  and cannot create an additional loss. The remaining intervals appear in both
  numerator and denominator. Bounding the ratio only by the ending weights
  therefore charges a connected interval once, including the final return to
  the base phase norm.
  An initial increase of the weight has no loss. Count a final return to
  $X_\lambda$ as an ending jump at $T$. Each connected interval ends once,
  giving~\eqref{E:interval-metric}. This proves Lemma~\ref{L:interval-complex}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Summing the coefficient bounds}

We first record an elementary bound for power series. The proof of
Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients} uses it to sum over the ways a total
order $k$ splits among the interval fields.

\LeanMarker{L:inventory-product}
\begin{lemma}[A coefficient bound for a product with nonnegative coefficients]\label{L:inventory-product}
  For $k \ge 1$, define
  \[
    v_i=i^{-\beta}+k^{-1}i^{-(\beta-1)}
    +k^{\alpha-2}i^{-p},\qquad
    b_{i,l}=\frac{i^{-\beta l}(1+li/k)^2}{(l!)^\nu},\quad l\ge2.
  \]
  There is a finite $C = C(\alpha,\alpha_0)$, independent of $M,k$, such that
  \begin{equation}\label{E:inventory-product}
    [z^k]\prod_{i=1}^M\left(1+v_i z+\sum_{l\ge2}b_{i,l}z^l\right)
    \le\frac{C^k}{(k!)^\nu}.
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We evaluate the positive generating product at one order-dependent radius.
  We separate the third singleton term, then estimate the coarse and fine
  interval indices. Positivity converts the product bound back into a
  coefficient bound.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We separate and bound the third singleton
  contribution.
  Evaluate the positive product at $z = \theta k^\nu$, with $\theta = 1/4$. Put
  $P_i = \theta k^\nu i^{-\beta}$ and $Q_i = \theta k i^{-p}$. The third
  singleton term is $Q_i$ because $\nu+\alpha-2 = 1$. The inequality $1+A+B \le
  (1+A)(1+B)$ separates the factors $1+Q_i$. For $p > 1$,
  \[
    \sum_{i\ge1}\log(1+A i^{-p})
    \le\int_0^\infty\log(1+A x^{-p})\ud x
    =A^{1/p}\int_0^\infty\log(1+x^{-p})\ud x.
  \]
  The last integral is finite. Hence, their logarithmic contribution is at most
  $Ck^{1/p} \le Ck$.

  The integral of $\log(1+x^{-p})$ is finite because its growth near zero is
  bounded by a constant plus $p|\log x|$, while at infinity it is at most
  $x^{-p}$. Both are integrable in their respective regions when $p>1$. This
  makes the singleton logarithmic contribution uniform in the number of
  factors.

  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We estimate the coarse factors by the factorial
  series.
  If $i \le k$, the remaining factor is at most $S_\nu(4P_i) =
  \sum_{l\ge0}(4P_i)^l/(l!)^\nu$: its singleton is at most $2P_i$, and
  $(1+li/k)^2 \le (1+l)^2 \le 4^l$. Since $\nu > 1$, $S_\nu(x) \le e^{\nu
  x^{1/\nu}}$. Therefore,
  \[
    \sum_{i\le k}\log S_\nu(4P_i)
    \le\nu(4\theta)^{1/\nu}k\sum_{i\ge1}i^{-\beta/\nu}
    \le Ck,
  \]
  because $\beta/\nu > 1$.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We sum the fine-index tails and extract the
  coefficient.
  If $i > k$, then $P_i \le \theta$. The remaining singleton tail is at most
  \[
    \theta\left(\frac1{\beta-1}+\frac1{\beta-2}\right)
    k^{\nu+1-\beta}=Ck^{\alpha_0}.
  \]
  Also, uniformly for $0 \le P \le 1/4$ and $y \ge 0$,
  $\sum_{l\ge2}P^l(1+ly)^2/(l!)^\nu \le CP^2(1+y^2)$. Summing this bound gives
  \[
    Ck^{2\nu}\left(\frac{k^{1-2\beta}}{2\beta-1}
    +\frac{k^{-2}k^{3-2\beta}}{2\beta-3}\right)
    =Ck^{2\alpha_0-1}\le Ck.
  \]
  Use $\log(1+y) \le y$ for these fine factors. The entire product is
  consequently at most $e^{Ck}$. Positivity of its coefficients bounds the $k$th
  coefficient by $\theta^{-k}k^{-\nu k}e^{Ck} \le C^k/(k!)^\nu$. This proves
  Lemma~\ref{L:inventory-product}.
\end{proof}

Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a finite family of connected intervals $I_i$ meeting
$[0,T]$, with lengths $h_i$ sorted decreasingly. Let $W_i^\epsilon$ be
independent copies of the smoothed unit field, and suppose
\begin{equation}\label{E:interval-inventory}
  h_i\le L T/i,\qquad \sigma_i^2\le c h_i^{-\alpha_0}.
\end{equation}
Write $\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}^\epsilon(T;s)$ for phase evolution with
potential $s\sum_i\sigma_i\mathbf1_{I_i}(t)W_i^\epsilon(x)$.

\input{notes/figures/interval-allocations}

\LeanMarker{L:fixed-split}
\begin{lemma}[A fixed degree-split estimate]\label{L:fixed-split}
  Retain the finite interval fields above, with $R_\alpha > 0$ and $\sigma_i >
  0$. Fix integers $r_i,q_i,k_i \ge 0$ with $r_i+q_i = 2k_i$, and put $k =
  \sum_i k_i \ge 1$, $\lambda = k/T$. Represent $W_i^\epsilon(x) =
  \sum_jg_{ij}e_{ij}(x)$ as in Lemma~\ref{L:interval-contractions}. Retain $m_i$
  Gaussian modes, a spatial Fourier band $S_b$, and Hermite cutoff $N_i = 2k_i$
  on color $i$. Write $P = \bigotimes_iP_{N_i}$ and set
  \[
    B_i=P S_b\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}g_{ij}M_{e_{ij}}\right)S_bP,
    \qquad V_{\boldsymbol z}(t)
    =\sum_i z_i\sigma_i\mathbf1_{I_i}(t)B_i.
  \]
  Let $\mathcal{M}^{\rm cut}_{\boldsymbol z}(T)$ be the phase evolution with
  this bounded potential and $A_0 = -\Delta$ on the band. Define $U_{\boldsymbol
  r}v = [\boldsymbol z^{\boldsymbol r}] \mathcal{M}^{\rm cut}_{\boldsymbol
  z}(T)v$, with the projected deterministic initial phase in the Gaussian
  vacuum. Put
  \[
    \begin{gathered}
      \mathcal{I}=\{i:k_i>0\},\qquad
      \mathcal{C}=\{i:k_i=1,\ r_i=q_i=1\},\\
      \mathcal{B}=\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{C},\qquad
      \ell_i=\mathbf1_{\{r_i>0\}}+\mathbf1_{\{q_i>0\}}.
    \end{gathered}
  \]
  There is a finite $C_{\rm split} = C_{\rm split}(\alpha)$, uniform in all
  these cutoffs and in the finite interval family, such that
  \begin{equation}\label{E:fixed-split-bound}
    \begin{split}
      |\langle U_{\boldsymbol r}v,U_{\boldsymbol q}v
      \rangle_{L^2(\Omega;X_{1/T})}|
      \le{}&k^2 C_{\rm split}^k R_\alpha^k\|v\|_{1/T}^2
      \prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}
      (\sigma_i^2h_i^2\lambda^{\alpha-2})\\
      &\quad\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{B}}
      \frac{(\sigma_i^2h_i^{4-\alpha})^{k_i}}{(k_i!)^\nu}
      (1+k_i/(\lambda h_i))^{\ell_i}.
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  In particular, a singleton used twice on one copy has $\ell_i = 1$; a higher
  color has at most two metric factors.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We estimate each vector coefficient on bounded cutoffs before pairing the
  two copies. We bound background segments on one common partition, count
  every change of phase norm once, and insert each cross color only from its
  vacuum. A cube integral and normalized vector Cauchy formula then give the
  coefficient with no extra permutation factor.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We set the active colors and background amplitude
  radii.
  We give a bound for each vector coefficient before taking their inner product.
  Every operator below acts on the finite Gaussian-mode, Hermite and
  spatial-band Hilbert space in the statement. In particular, it is bounded. Its
  piecewise-constant evolution is a finite product of block exponentials, hence
  is entire in every amplitude. All coefficient extractions in this proof are
  ordinary power-series extractions.

  An amplitude with $k_i = 0$ is evaluated at zero on both copies. If $k_i > 0$
  but $I_i\cap[0,T]$ has zero length, every coefficient with a positive power of
  that amplitude vanishes. The claimed inner product is then zero. We may
  therefore assume that every active interval has a positive-length intersection
  $I_i^T = (a_i,b_i)$ with $(0,T)$; endpoint conventions do not affect the
  evolution or its time integrals. Its length is at most $h_i$.

  Write $a = \alpha/2$, and use the positive constant $\delta_a$ in
  Lemma~\ref{L:complex-phase}. For every $i \in \mathcal{B}$ put
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-radii}
    \omega_i=\frac{k_i}{h_i},\qquad
    d_i=2\sqrt{2k_i+1}\sqrt{R_\alpha},\qquad
    \varrho_i=\frac{\delta_a\omega_i^{2-a}}{\sigma_i d_i}.
  \end{equation}
  Fix one of the two copies. Its background set is $\mathcal{A}_r =
  \{i\in\mathcal{B}:r_i>0\}$ or $\mathcal{A}_q = \{i\in\mathcal{B}:q_i>0\}$;
  denote that set by $\mathcal{A}$. Evaluate every unused amplitude at zero.
  Colors in $\mathcal{C}$ have degree one on this copy. Put $m = |\mathcal{C}|$.
  Thus, there are at most $k$ background and cross colors altogether.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We bound background evolution on the common
  chronological partition.
  \emph{Background segments and the common partition.} Fix $|z_i| = \varrho_i$
  for $i \in \mathcal{A}$. With the cross amplitudes zero, the potential is
  $V_z(t) = \sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}z_i\sigma_i\mathbf1_{I_i^T}(t)B_i$. Write
  $P_z(t,s)$ for its evolution, and set
  \[
    \Lambda(t)=\lambda+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}
    \omega_i\mathbf1_{I_i^T}(t).
  \]
  For each $\rho > 0$, Lemma~\ref{L:interval-contractions} gives
  \[
    \|A_\rho^{-1/2}V_z(t)A_\rho^{-1/2}\|
    \le D(t)\rho^{a-2},\qquad
    D(t)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|z_i|\sigma_i d_i
    \mathbf1_{I_i^T}(t)
    \le\delta_a\Lambda(t)^{2-a}.
  \]
  The last step follows from~\eqref{E:split-radii} and $\sum x_i^{2-a} \le (\sum
  x_i)^{2-a}$ for nonnegative $x_i$. The spatial resolvents here include the
  identity on every Gaussian factor. This is a deterministic operator inequality
  on their joint Hilbert space; the input can depend on the background fields.

  For the moment fix a tuple $(t_i)_{i\in\mathcal{C}}$ with $t_i \in I_i^T$, all
  distinct and away from the background endpoints. The excluded tuples have
  Lebesgue measure zero. Form the common partition
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-partition}
    0=\tau_0<\tau_1<\cdots<\tau_M=T
  \end{equation}
  from these times and all background endpoints in $(0,T)$. On
  $(\tau_{j-1},\tau_j)$ let $\Lambda_j$ be the constant value of $\Lambda$, and
  put $P_j = P_z(\tau_j,\tau_{j-1})$. There are at most $2|\mathcal{A}|+m$
  interior partition points, so $M \le 2|\mathcal{A}|+m+1 \le 3k+1$.
  Lemma~\ref{L:interval-complex}, with its adjoint bound as stated there, gives
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-segment-product}
    \begin{split}
      \prod_{j=1}^M\|P_j\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda_j}\to
      \mathcal{H}_{\Lambda_j}}
      &\le100^M\exp\left(2\sum_{j=1}^M
      \Lambda_j(\tau_j-\tau_{j-1})\right)\\
      &\le100^{3k+1}e^{4k}=:M_k,
      \qquad \mathcal{H}_\rho=L^2(\Omega;X_\rho).
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  Indeed, the sum in the exponential is exactly $\lambda
  T+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\omega_i(b_i-a_i) \le k+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}k_i \le
  2k$. Insertion times refine a partition; they do not repeat this integral.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We count the phase-metric changes.
  \emph{Every identity between phase norms.} Put $\Lambda_0 = \Lambda_{M+1} =
  \lambda$. At $\tau_j$ let $\mathscr{I}_j$ be the identity from
  $\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda_j}$ to $\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda_{j+1}}$, for $0 \le j \le
  M$. Its norm is at most
  \[
    q_j:=\max\{1,\Lambda_j/\Lambda_{j+1}\}.
  \]
  This follows directly from the two components of the phase norm and the
  spectral inequality $A_{\Lambda_{j+1}}^{-1} \le q_j^2 A_{\Lambda_j}^{-1}$. At
  an interior background endpoint, let $E_j,S_j,R_j$ denote the ending, starting
  and remaining background colors. Then
  \[
    \frac{\Lambda_j}{\Lambda_{j+1}}
    =\frac{\lambda+\sum_{R_j}\omega_i+\sum_{E_j}\omega_i}
    {\lambda+\sum_{R_j}\omega_i+\sum_{S_j}\omega_i}
    \le1+\sum_{E_j}\omega_i/\lambda
    \le\prod_{i\in E_j}(1+\omega_i/\lambda).
  \]
  The last product is at least one, so it also bounds $q_j$. At an
  insertion-only cut the heights agree and $q_j = 1$. Initially $q_0 = 1$. At
  $T$ the same inequality charges all intervals with $b_i = T$ as ending there.
  Each connected $I_i^T$ ends at precisely one such cut. Consequently,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-copy-metric}
    \prod_{j=0}^M q_j
    \le D_{\mathcal{A}}:=
    \prod_{i\in\mathcal{A}}(1+k_i/(\lambda h_i)).
  \end{equation}
  This product includes the initial and final conversions. There is no new copy
  of $D_{\mathcal{A}}$ at an insertion time or an overlapping interval's start.

  \input{notes/figures/interval-end-metrics}

  Insertion times do not change the active background weight. They only split
  an interval of integration in the exponential bound, and their metric ratio
  is one. Thus, neither the integrated height nor the product of ending
  charges is repeated when a cross-color insertion refines the partition.

  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 4.~}We estimate every cross-color insertion on its
  actual tensor subspace.
  \emph{The cross-color subspaces and the word norm.} Let $\mathscr{G}_i^{(0)}$
  be the vacuum of color $i$ and $\mathscr{G}_i^{(1)}$ its first Gaussian
  degree. For $S \subset \mathcal{C}$, use the tensor subspace
  \[
    \mathscr{Z}_S=
    \bigotimes_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\mathscr{G}_i^{\le N_i}
    \otimes\bigotimes_{i\in S}\mathscr{G}_i^{(1)}
    \otimes\bigotimes_{i\in\mathcal{C}\setminus S}
    \mathscr{G}_i^{(0)},
  \]
  with every omitted Gaussian color in its vacuum and with the common spatial
  band understood. Each $P_j$ preserves the corresponding phase space over
  $\mathscr{Z}_S$: its potential acts only on colors in $\mathcal{A}$, and its
  spatial operators act identically on all cross Gaussian factors. The
  identities $\mathscr{I}_j$ preserve the same tensor subspace.

  Define $J_i(u,w) = (0,\sigma_i B_i u)$ for $i \in \mathcal{C}$. If $i\notin
  S$, multiplication by its field takes its vacuum into first Gaussian degree.
  The Hermite cutoff $N_i = 2$ does not alter this step. Thus, $J_i$ maps the
  phase over $\mathscr{Z}_S$ into the phase over $\mathscr{Z}_{S\cup\{i\}}$.
  Collect all other Gaussian factors into $\mathscr{R}_i$. Every input has the
  form $1_i\otimes(u,w)$, with $(u,w) \in \mathscr{R}_i\otimes X_\rho$. The
  cutoffs on other colors are identities on its retained degrees, which
  multiplication by the color-$i$ field leaves unchanged. Hence,
  \[
    J_i(1_i\otimes(u,w))
    =\sigma_i\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}g_{ij}\otimes
    (0,S_bM_{e_{ij}}S_bu).
  \]
  Orthogonality $\E[g_{ij}g_{il}] = \mathbf1_{\{j=l\}}$ gives
  \[
    \begin{split}
      \|J_i(1_i\otimes(u,w))\|_{\mathcal{H}_\rho}^2
      &=\sigma_i^2\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
      \|A_\rho^{-1/2}S_bM_{e_{ij}}S_bu
      \|_{\mathscr{R}_i\otimes L^2_x}^2\\
      &\le\sigma_i^2R_\alpha\rho^{\alpha-2}
      \|u\|_{\mathscr{R}_i\otimes L^2_x}^2\\
      &\le\sigma_i^2R_\alpha\rho^{\alpha-2}
      \|1_i\otimes(u,w)\|_{\mathcal{H}_\rho}^2.
    \end{split}
  \]
  The first inequality is~\eqref{E:interval-column} tensored with
  $\mathscr{R}_i$ and allows arbitrary dependence on earlier colors. Thus, at
  the insertion time,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-fresh-insertion}
    \|J_i\|\le\sigma_i\sqrt{R_\alpha}
    \Lambda(t_i)^{(\alpha-2)/2}
    \le c_i:=\sigma_i\sqrt{R_\alpha}\lambda^{(\alpha-2)/2}.
  \end{equation}
  Starting with $\mathscr{Z}_\varnothing$, these subspaces describe every
  partial word. They justify each new-color estimate even though the current
  vector depends on the background and earlier cross colors. No occupied-color
  annihilation is estimated by a vacuum bound.

  Equivalently, apply Lemma~\ref{L:finite-word-cutoff} to the cross colors, with
  $\mathscr{X}$ containing the bounded background Gaussian space and the spatial
  phase space. Background segments act only on $\mathscr{X}$, and each cross
  color occurs once. Its cutoff is an exact identity on every prefix of the
  selected word. This does not identify an untruncated background propagator
  with a fixed cutoff.

  At an interior cut $\tau_j$ let $B_j^{\rm cut} = J_i$ if $\tau_j = t_i$ and
  let $B_j^{\rm cut}$ be the identity otherwise. Apply $B_j^{\rm cut}$ after
  $\mathscr{I}_j$; at an insertion cut the two heights are equal. The
  chronological word, as a map from $\mathcal{H}_\lambda$ back to
  $\mathcal{H}_\lambda$, is exactly
  \[
    \mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,\boldsymbol t)
    =\mathscr{I}_M P_M B_{M-1}^{\rm cut}\mathscr{I}_{M-1}P_{M-1}
    \cdots B_1^{\rm cut}\mathscr{I}_1P_1\mathscr{I}_0.
  \]
  For $M = 1$ this means $\mathscr{I}_1P_1\mathscr{I}_0$. The rightmost factor
  acts first; no spatial operators are commuted. Multiplying their norms, using
  the subspaces just specified, gives the explicit bound
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-word-product}
    \begin{split}
      \|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,\boldsymbol t)v\|_\lambda
      &\le\left(\prod_{j=1}^M\|P_j\|\right)
      \left(\prod_{j=0}^M q_j\right)
      \left(\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}c_i\right)\|v\|_\lambda\\
      &\le M_kD_{\mathcal{A}}\|v\|_\lambda
      \prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}c_i.
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  The background norm bound is on the joint Gaussian Hilbert space; it is not
  obtained by conditioning on successive fields.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 5.~}We integrate over the insertion cube and extract
  vector coefficients.
  \emph{The insertion cube and vector Cauchy formula.} Give the cross colors
  amplitudes $y_i$ and define the vector
  \[
    \Phi_{\mathcal{A}}(z)
    :=\left[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}y_i\right]
    \mathcal{M}^{\rm cut}_{(z,y,0)}(T)v.
  \]
  Variation of constants about $P_z$ and iteration give a Dyson series. A
  monomial of degree one in every $y_i$ uses exactly $m$ insertions, with each
  distinct cross color used once. Hence,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-cross-cube}
    \Phi_{\mathcal{A}}(z)
    =\sum_{\pi}\int_{D_\pi}
    \mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,\boldsymbol t)v\,\ud\boldsymbol t,
    \qquad
    D_\pi=\left\{\boldsymbol t\in\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}I_i^T:
    t_{\pi_1}<\cdots<t_{\pi_m}\right\}.
  \end{equation}
  Each ordered Dyson integral has coefficient one. The permutation simplices
  $D_\pi$ partition the cube up to null diagonals. In particular,
  \[
    \sum_\pi |D_\pi|=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}|I_i^T|
    \le\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}h_i.
  \]
  Minkowski applied to~\eqref{E:interval-cross-cube} and the single-word
  bound~\eqref{E:split-word-product} therefore gives
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-cross-vector}
    \|\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\|_\lambda
    \le M_kD_{\mathcal{A}}\|v\|_\lambda
    \prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}c_i h_i.
  \end{equation}
  There is no factor $m!$ or $1/m!$ in this estimate. For $m = 0$, the cube has
  unit mass and $\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}(z) = P_z(T,0)v$.

  \input{notes/figures/cross-colour-cube}

  Each time tuple with distinct entries has exactly one chronological
  ordering, so it belongs to exactly one of the permutation simplices. The sum
  of their volumes is the cube volume, not that volume multiplied by the
  number of permutations. This is why the new-color insertion estimate has no
  factorial in the number of cross colors.
  The vector $\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}$ is entire in the background amplitudes. On the
  first copy its coefficient is given by the normalized formula
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-vector-cauchy}
    \begin{split}
      U_{\boldsymbol r}v
      ={}&\left(\prod_{i\in\mathcal{A}_r}\varrho_i^{-r_i}\right)
      \int_{[0,2\pi]^{\mathcal{A}_r}}
      e^{-i\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}_r}r_i\theta_i}
      \Phi_{\mathcal{A}_r}((\varrho_i e^{i\theta_i})_i)
      \prod_{i\in\mathcal{A}_r}\frac{\ud\theta_i}{2\pi}.
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  This is precisely the desired coefficient because each cross exponent equals
  one and every omitted exponent is zero. The normalized angular measure has
  total mass one. Thus,~\eqref{E:interval-cross-vector} bounds $\|U_{\boldsymbol
  r}v\|_\lambda$ with the additional factor
  $\prod_{i\in\mathcal{A}_r}\varrho_i^{-r_i}$, and the identical formula bounds
  the second copy using $\mathcal{A}_q,q_i$. No holomorphic formula is applied
  to a squared norm.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 6.~}We pair the two coefficients and account for all
  constants.
  \emph{The inner product and all constants.} Since $\lambda = k/T$ and $k \ge
  1$,
  \[
    \|w\|_{1/T}\le k\|w\|_\lambda,\qquad
    \|v\|_\lambda\le\|v\|_{1/T}.
  \]
  Cauchy--Schwarz between the two vectors on the same Gaussian space now gives,
  before any constants are absorbed,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-before-absorption}
    \begin{split}
      |\langle U_{\boldsymbol r}v,U_{\boldsymbol q}v
      \rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1/T}}|
      \le{}&k^2 M_k^2D_{\mathcal{A}_r}D_{\mathcal{A}_q}
      \|v\|_{1/T}^2
      \prod_{i\in\mathcal{B}}\varrho_i^{-2k_i}\\
      &\quad\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}}
      (R_\alpha\sigma_i^2h_i^2\lambda^{\alpha-2}).
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  Both coefficients live on the same Gaussian space; their inner product is
  bounded by Cauchy--Schwarz, not by independence of the copies. The amplitude
  radius contributes once for each occurrence in either coefficient, giving
  total exponent $r_i+q_i=2k_i$. The metric charge appears only on copies
  where that color is present.
  The radius exponent is $r_i+q_i = 2k_i$. The metric product is exactly
  \[
    D_{\mathcal{A}_r}D_{\mathcal{A}_q}
    =\prod_{i\in\mathcal{B}}(1+k_i/(\lambda h_i))^{\ell_i}.
  \]
  In particular, a singleton on only one copy has one ending charge, not two.
  For every $i \in \mathcal{B}$, $d_i^2 \le 12k_iR_\alpha$ gives
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-radius-factorial}
    \begin{split}
      \varrho_i^{-2k_i}
      &=\left(\frac{\sigma_i^2d_i^2}{\delta_a^2}\right)^{k_i}
      \left(\frac{h_i}{k_i}\right)^{(4-\alpha)k_i}\\
      &\le(12\delta_a^{-2})^{k_i}
      (R_\alpha\sigma_i^2h_i^{4-\alpha})^{k_i}
      k_i^{-(3-\alpha)k_i}\\
      &\le(12\delta_a^{-2})^{k_i}
      \frac{(R_\alpha\sigma_i^2h_i^{4-\alpha})^{k_i}}
      {(k_i!)^{3-\alpha}}.
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  The last step uses $k_i! \le k_i^{k_i}$. Finally,
  \[
    M_k^2=100^{6k+2}e^{8k}\le(100^8e^8)^k,
    \qquad |\mathcal{C}|+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}}k_i=k.
  \]
  Substituting these inequalities in~\eqref{E:split-before-absorption}
  proves~\eqref{E:fixed-split-bound} with the explicit admissible choice
  \begin{equation}\label{E:split-explicit-constant}
    C_{\rm split}=100^8e^8
    \max\{1,12\delta_{\alpha/2}^{-2}\}.
  \end{equation}
  This constant depends only on $\alpha$. No permutation, Gaussian-mode, factor
  depending on the number of intervals or further degree factor remains. The
  projected initial phase has norm at most the displayed original $\|v\|_{1/T}$.
  This proves Lemma~\ref{L:fixed-split}.
\end{proof}

\LeanMarker{P:interval-coefficients}
\begin{proposition}[Uniform interval coefficients]\label{P:interval-coefficients}
  There is $C = C(\alpha,\alpha_0,L,c)$ such that, for real Schwartz phase $v$,
  every $k \ge 1$ satisfies
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-coefficient-bound}
    \left|[s^{2k}]\E\|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}^\epsilon(T;s)v\|_{1/T}^2\right|
    \le\frac{C^k R_\alpha^k T^{\beta k}}{(k!)^\nu}\|v\|_{1/T}^2,
  \end{equation}
  uniformly in $\epsilon > 0$ and the finite family. In particular,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-phase-bound}
    \E\|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}^\epsilon(T;1)v\|_{1/T}^2
    \le e\exp\{C R_\alpha^{1/\nu}T^{\beta/\nu}\}\|v\|_{1/T}^2.
  \end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  We first construct the ordinary coefficient series at fixed smoothing and
  interval family. We retain only even total degree in each color and use
  exact finite-word cutoffs. The fixed-split estimate and sorted interval
  lengths reduce the uniform sum to the inventory product, after which
  separate cutoff limits identify and sum the full series.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We set up the bounded cutoffs and entire vector
  series.
  Zero noise is trivial; omit any zero $\sigma_i$. Enlarge the interval-length
  constant $L$ to $\max\{1,L\}$ if necessary. The hypotheses remain true and the
  final constant may depend on $L$, so throughout this proof we may assume $L
  \ge 1$. Replacing $(\sigma_i,W_i^\epsilon)$ by
  $(|\sigma_i|,\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_i)W_i^\epsilon)$ leaves the potential
  unchanged and preserves the joint law of the independent real Gaussian fields.
  Thus, we may assume $\sigma_i > 0$. We first work with finitely many Gaussian
  coordinates of each field and a bounded spatial Fourier band. Denote their
  common phase Hilbert space by $\mathcal{H}_\rho = L^2(\Omega;X_\rho)$. The
  initial data are projected to the band. This contracts every $X_\rho$ norm; we
  keep the original $\|v\|_\rho$ on the right of the estimates. The projections
  converge strongly to the original data.

  \emph{Ordinary coefficients and their existence.} Fix $\lambda > 0$
  temporarily. Give interval $i$ its own amplitude $z_i$ and write
  \[
    \mathcal{U}(T;\boldsymbol z)v
    =\sum_{\boldsymbol r\in\N_0^{\mathcal{F}}}
    \boldsymbol z^{\boldsymbol r}U_{\boldsymbol r}v.
  \]
  These are ordinary power-series coefficients, without factorials incorporated
  in their definition. At fixed smoothing and finite interval family, put
  $v_{\max} = \sup_t\sum_i\sigma_i^2\mathbf1_{I_i}(t) \Gamma_\epsilon(0) <
  \infty$. On Gaussian degrees at most $n$, multiplication by the instantaneous
  field has norm at most $2\sqrt{n+1}\sqrt{v_{\max}}$. The free-phase energy
  calculation at the end of the proof of
  Proposition~\ref{P:static-phase-variance}, which uses no dimension-specific
  or covariance assumption, gives the bound $e^{\lambda h/2}$ in $X_\lambda$
  also on each spatial Fourier band. A phase insertion
  $(u,w)\mapsto(0,Wu)$ costs at most its unweighted multiplication norm divided
  by $\lambda$. Thus, on $\max_i|z_i| \le z_*$ the total order-$n$ phase is
  bounded by
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-entire-majorant}
    e^{\lambda T/2}
    \frac{(2z_*\sqrt{n+1}\sqrt{v_{\max}}T/\lambda)^n}{n!}
    \|v\|_\lambda .
  \end{equation}
  The series of these bounds converges for every finite $z_*$. Its locally
  uniform convergence defines an entire vector function. We also verify
  coefficientwise absolute convergence. Fix $z_* > 0$ and put
  \[
    K_{2z_*}=\sup_{\max_i|z_i|\le2z_*}
    \|\mathcal{U}(T;\boldsymbol z)v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1/T}}<\infty,
  \]
  using the majorant with its temporary parameter $\lambda = 1/T$. Normalized
  vector Cauchy integration on this larger polydisc gives
  \[
    \|U_{\boldsymbol r}v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1/T}}
    \le K_{2z_*}(2z_*)^{-|\boldsymbol r|},\qquad
    \sum_{\boldsymbol r}z_*^{|\boldsymbol r|}
    \|U_{\boldsymbol r}v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1/T}}
    \le2^{|\mathcal{F}|}K_{2z_*}.
  \]
  The sum is the product of $|\mathcal{F}|$ geometric series. Cauchy--Schwarz
  therefore bounds the absolute energy double series by
  $4^{|\mathcal{F}|}K_{2z_*}^2$. This finite factor justifies the coefficient
  identities at fixed family and smoothing; it does not enter the uniform
  coefficient estimate below.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We identify the energy coefficient and its color
  splits.
  Set $Q_{\boldsymbol r,\boldsymbol q} = \langle U_{\boldsymbol
  r}v,U_{\boldsymbol q}v \rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1/T}}$. Both vectors use the same
  Gaussian fields. Independence is between distinct colors, not between the two
  copies in this inner product. For real $s$, substitution $z_i = s$ gives the
  exact identity
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-energy-splits}
    [s^{2k}]\E\|\mathcal{U}(T;s)v\|_{1/T}^2
    =\sum_{|\boldsymbol r|+|\boldsymbol q|=2k}
    Q_{\boldsymbol r,\boldsymbol q}.
  \end{equation}
  Reflection of Gaussian color $i$ multiplies $U_{\boldsymbol r}v$ by
  $(-1)^{r_i}$. Hence, $Q_{\boldsymbol r,\boldsymbol q} = 0$ unless $r_i+q_i =
  2k_i$ for every $i$, where $\sum_i k_i = k$. If $k_i = 0$, both coefficients
  are obtained by setting $z_i = 0$; this removes that field exactly. There are
  at most $k$ active colors. For fixed $\boldsymbol k$, the number of splits is
  $\prod_i(2k_i+1) \le 3^k$.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We retain the selected coefficients exactly at
  finite Hermite degrees.
  For this fixed $\boldsymbol k$, truncate the Hermite degree of color $i$ at
  $N_i = 2k_i$. A word contributing to $U_{\boldsymbol r}$ has $r_i \le 2k_i$
  insertions of color $i$, and the other copy has $q_i \le 2k_i$.
  Lemma~\ref{L:finite-word-cutoff}, with the spatial phase space as
  $\mathscr{X}$ and free phase factors between insertions, therefore preserves
  both selected vector coefficients exactly. Their Duhamel time integrals and
  finite word sums preserve the equality. Creation followed by annihilation is
  retained; there is no Hermite-degree limit in this coefficient calculation.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 4.~}We convert the split estimate into the interval
  inventory weights.
  For the uniform coefficient estimate put $\lambda = k/T$. Apply
  Lemma~\ref{L:fixed-split} to each selected coefficient, retaining the original
  sorted interval labels in the final sum. Its factor $\ell_i$ is one for a
  singleton mean and at most two for every higher color. The separate cross
  factor applies only at $(r_i,q_i) = (1,1)$. Absorb $k^2C_{\rm split}^k3^kc^k$
  into $C^k$, using $k^2 \le 4^k$. After $\sigma_i^2 \le c h_i^{-\alpha_0}$, the
  resulting local weights, with this common per-total-order constant understood,
  are
  \[
    \begin{array}{c|c}
      \text{case}&\text{weight in energy}\\ \hline
      (2,0)\text{ or }(0,2)&R_\alpha h_i^\beta
      (1+1/(\lambda h_i))\\
      (1,1)&R_\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-2}h_i^{2-\alpha_0}\\
      k_i=l\ge2&R_\alpha^l h_i^{\beta l}
      (1+l/(\lambda h_i))^2/(l!)^\nu .
    \end{array}
  \]
  The first metric factor occurs on only one copy; the last costs at most one
  factor on each copy. To justify the length substitution, expand the entire
  higher-degree weight, before its positive constant:
  \[
    h^{\beta l}(1+l/(\lambda h))^2
    =h^{\beta l}+\frac{2l}{\lambda}h^{\beta l-1}
    +\frac{l^2}{\lambda^2}h^{\beta l-2}.
  \]
  Since $\beta > 2$ and $l \ge 2$, all three powers are positive. The singleton
  mean likewise has powers $\beta,\beta-1$, and the cross singleton has power $p
  = 2-\alpha_0 > 0$. Thus, each whole local weight increases with $h$; we do not
  bound the inverse-length factor separately. At $h = LT/i$, $1+l/(\lambda h) =
  1+li/(kL) \le 1+li/k$, since $L \ge 1$. The remaining powers of $L$ are
  absorbed in the allowed $C^k$. For a singleton let $m_i,c_i$ be the first and
  second table weights. Every one of its splits is bounded by $m_i+c_i$. The
  higher-degree table weight is already uniform over its splits. Hence, the
  previously counted $\prod_i(2k_i+1) \le 3^k$ bounds all split choices once,
  using these combined singleton weights; that is the factor absorbed into $C^k$
  above. Substituting $h_i \le LT/i$ and $\lambda = k/T$ therefore extracts
  $C^kR_\alpha^kT^{\beta k}$, leaving $v_i$ at $k_i = 1$ and $b_{i,l}$ at $k_i =
  l \ge 2$ from Lemma~\ref{L:inventory-product}.

  The inverse-length factor cannot be bounded separately when replacing $h_i$
  by its upper bound: it moves in the opposite direction. Expanding the whole
  weight gives only positive powers of $h_i$, so each term is increasing. This
  justifies the length substitution for the complete local weight before any
  constants are collected.

  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 5.~}We sum ordinary coefficients without a multinomial
  factor.
  There is no further multinomial factor: for nonnegative weights $w_{i,0} = 1$,
  ordinary multiplication of power series gives exactly
  \[
    \sum_{\sum_i k_i=k}\prod_i w_{i,k_i}
    =[z^k]\prod_i\left(\sum_{l\ge0}w_{i,l}z^l\right).
  \]
  Apply this identity to the sum in~\eqref{E:interval-energy-splits}, using the
  two singleton alternatives together in $v_i$. Lemma~\ref{L:inventory-product}
  now proves \eqref{E:interval-coefficient-bound} at the finite cutoffs.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 6.~}We remove cutoffs at fixed coefficients and sum
  the uniform bound.
  \emph{Identification and summation.} Keep positive smoothing, finite interval
  family and a fixed coefficient. The finite-degree multiplication bound used
  in~\eqref{E:interval-entire-majorant} is uniform in mode and band cutoffs. On
  finite-coordinate Gaussian vectors the omitted annihilation tail is zero and
  the creation tail tends to zero by its omitted pointwise variances and
  dominated convergence. Density in each finite degree extends this strong
  convergence. Closedness identifies the limit with multiplication by the
  measurable smoothed field. Fourier projections converge strongly and commute
  with free evolution. Dominated time integration thus identifies every fixed
  Duhamel word. The selected coefficients of the local Hermite truncation were
  already equal to those untruncated words. The full series is constructed
  separately using~\eqref{E:interval-entire-majorant}, choosing a sufficient
  degree cutoff anew at each order. A fixed-degree truncated propagator is not
  identified with the full evolution. The entire majorant gives convergence
  uniformly on compact amplitude sets. The coefficients estimated above are
  therefore those of the finite-interval model, with no zero-smoothing limit at
  this step.

  The fixed-family entire majorant establishes existence and identifies
  coefficients after the mode and band limits. The separate coefficient
  estimate is uniform in those parameters and in the interval family. Keeping
  these two roles distinct prevents the smoothing-dependent point variance
  from entering the final growth constant.
  The nonuniform number $v_{\max}$ was used only for this identification. The
  uniform coefficient bound contains no such number. Its constants come from
  $\delta_{\alpha/2}$, the fixed phase bound, the interval-length bound lemma
  and $L,c$; covariance enters only through $R_\alpha^k$. The zero-order energy
  is at most $e\|v\|_{1/T}^2$. Put $x = CR_\alpha T^\beta$ and $Y = e^{\nu
  x^{1/\nu}} \ge 1$. Since $\nu > 1$, $\sum_{k\ge0}x^k/(k!)^\nu \le Y$. Summing
  the coefficient bounds in absolute value gives
  \[
    \E\|\mathcal{U}(T;1)v\|_{1/T}^2
    \le(e+Y-1)\|v\|_{1/T}^2\le eY\|v\|_{1/T}^2.
  \]
  Absorbing $\nu C^{1/\nu}$ into the parameter-only constant proves
  \eqref{E:interval-phase-bound}. This proves
  Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Finite Gaussian models and their terminal kernels}

For $n \ge 1$, define the positive terminal-chain measure
\begin{equation}\label{E:terminal-chain-measure}
  \mathsf{g}_n^{T,x}(\ud\boldsymbol s,\ud\boldsymbol y)
  =\mathbf1_{[0,T]^n_<}(\boldsymbol s)\ud\boldsymbol s
  \prod_{j=1}^n
  G_{s_{j+1}-s_j}(y_{j+1}-\ud y_j),
  \qquad (s_{n+1},y_{n+1})=(T,x).
\end{equation}
The spatial product is read backwards from $x$. Explicitly, it is the
pushforward of $\prod_{j=1}^nG_{s_{j+1}-s_j}(\ud w_j)$ under $y_j =
x-\sum_{l=j}^n w_l$. This defines the measure also in dimension three. In
dimensions one and two its density is the ordered kernel $g_n$
in~\eqref{E:expansion}. Set $\mathsf{g}_0^{T,x}$ equal to unit mass on the empty
tuple. This definition introduces no kernel symmetrization.

\LeanMarker{L:terminal-wick}
\begin{lemma}[The finite terminal-chain Wick formula]\label{L:terminal-wick}
  Let $\mathcal{W}(s,y)$ be a centered real Gaussian field on $[0,T]\times\R^d$,
  $d = 1,2,3$, with covariance $\mathcal{K}(s,y;r,z)$. Suppose that $V =
  \sup_{s,y}\E|\mathcal{W}(s,y)|^2 < \infty$, and that the field is jointly
  continuous in mean square on each open cell of a finite partition of $[0,T]$,
  times $\R^d$. Define $Y_0 = 1$ and
  \[
    Y_n(T,x)=\int\prod_{j=1}^n\mathcal{W}(s_j,y_j)
    \,\mathsf{g}_n^{T,x}(\ud\boldsymbol s,\ud\boldsymbol y).
  \]
  These Bochner integrals exist in $L^2(\Omega)$ and satisfy
  \begin{equation}\label{E:terminal-term-majorant}
    \|Y_n(T,x)\|_2
    \le\sqrt{(2n-1)!!}\,V^{n/2}\frac{T^{2n}}{(2n)!},
    \qquad n\ge1.
  \end{equation}
  For $m+n = 2k$, concatenate $\boldsymbol\tau =
  (s_1,\ldots,s_m,r_1,\ldots,r_n)$ and $\boldsymbol\eta =
  (y_1,\ldots,y_m,z_1,\ldots,z_n)$. Then
  \begin{equation}\label{E:terminal-wick-formula}
    \E[Y_m(T,x)Y_n(T,x)]
    =\sum_{D\in\Pi_k}\mathcal{T}_{m,n,D}^{\mathcal{K}}(T,x),
  \end{equation}
  where, with both chains terminating at $(T,x)$,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:terminal-pairing-integral}
    \begin{split}
      \mathcal{T}_{m,n,D}^{\mathcal{K}}
      ={}&\int_{[0,T]^m_<\times[0,T]^n_<}
      \ud\boldsymbol s\,\ud\boldsymbol r
      \int_{(\R^d)^m\times(\R^d)^n}\\
      &\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^m
      G_{s_{j+1}-s_j}(y_{j+1}-\ud y_j)
      \prod_{j=1}^n
      G_{r_{j+1}-r_j}(z_{j+1}-\ud z_j)\\
      &\quad\times\prod_{\{a,b\}\in D}
      \mathcal{K}(\tau_a,\eta_a;\tau_b,\eta_b).
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  Each pairing has coefficient one. If $m+n$ is odd, the expectation is zero.
  Empty chains and the case $m = n = 0$ have the usual empty-product meaning. If
  $\mathcal{K} \ge 0$, every integral above is nonnegative. Consequently,
  entrywise comparison $0 \le \mathcal{K}_1 \le \mathcal{K}_2$ compares all
  finite partial-order second moments in the same direction. The series
  $\sum_{n\ge0}r^nY_n$ converges absolutely in $L^2$ for every real or complex
  $r$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We establish the finite chain mass and Gaussian product integrability first.
  We then derive the labelled Wick formula and check symmetrization and Green
  smoothing. Positivity compares finite moments, while the explicit ratio test
  sums every fixed regularized series.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We prove Bochner integrability of each terminal
  chain.
  The mass of $G_h$ is $h$. If $M_n(T)$ is the total mass of
  $\mathsf{g}_n^{T,x}$, integration at the last vertex gives $M_0 = 1$ and
  $M_n(T) = \int_0^T(T-s)M_{n-1}(s)\ud s$. Induction therefore yields $M_n(T) =
  T^{2n}/(2n)!$. Gaussian moments and H\"older's inequality give
  \[
    \left\|\prod_{j=1}^n\mathcal{W}(s_j,y_j)\right\|_2
    \le\prod_{j=1}^n\|\mathcal{W}(s_j,y_j)\|_{2n}
    \le\sqrt{(2n-1)!!}\,V^{n/2}.
  \]
  Mean-square continuity of Gaussian variables implies their continuity in every
  fixed finite $L^p$. Hence, the product is strongly measurable in $L^2$ on the
  stated pieces. The finite kernel mass and the displayed bound prove Bochner
  integrability and~\eqref{E:terminal-term-majorant}.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We derive the coefficient-one Wick formula.
  Fubini now passes expectation through the product of the two finite kernel
  measures. For the $m+n$ Gaussian variables at their labelled vertices,
  differentiate
  \[
    \E\exp\left(\sum_i t_i\mathcal{W}(\tau_i,\eta_i)\right)
    =\exp\left(\frac12\sum_{i,j}t_i t_j
    \mathcal{K}(\tau_i,\eta_i;\tau_j,\eta_j)\right)
  \]
  once in each $t_i$ at zero. Odd total degree gives zero. At degree $2k$ the
  result is the sum over unordered pair partitions, with one covariance product
  per pairing. Orientations and orderings of the $k$ pairs cancel the
  exponential's $2^k k!$ denominator. Integration
  gives~\eqref{E:terminal-wick-formula} with precisely the domains and
  coefficients in~\eqref{E:terminal-pairing-integral}.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We retain the ordered convention and the
  Green-width limit.
  Symmetrizing a chain measure does not change its stochastic integral: the
  product of its $n$ scalar field values is invariant under relabeling. Thus,
  averaging the $n!$ pushforwards of $\mathsf{g}_n^{T,x}$ with coefficient
  $1/n!$ gives the same $Y_n$. There is no additional $n!$ factor
  in~\eqref{E:terminal-wick-formula}, or $m!n!$ in a two-chain product. This
  identifies the ordinary ordered regularized integrals and their symmetrized
  convention in~\eqref{E:expansion}.

  In dimension three one may first replace $G$ by $p_\eta*G$. Those positive
  measures have the same masses and converge weakly to $G$. Off the finitely
  many time cut points, all finite Gaussian moments are bounded continuous
  functions of the spatial variables. Dominated convergence for their finite
  Wick sums proves the same limit for the mixed second moments with two Green
  widths. It gives $L^2$ convergence to the Bochner integral above, exactly the
  fixed-noise Green convention used in the source.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 4.~}We compare positive diagrams and sum the
  regularized orders.
  If $\mathcal{K} \ge 0$, positivity of the Green measures makes each covariance
  product nonnegative. Comparing them proves the stated finite-moment
  monotonicity, including self-pairs and unequal chains. Finally, the ratio of
  successive right sides of~\eqref{E:terminal-term-majorant}, with an additional
  factor $|r|^n$, is $|r|\sqrt V T^2/((2n+2)\sqrt{2n+1})$, which tends to zero.
  The zero-variance case is immediate. This proves Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Removing the regularization}

Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients} holds uniformly in the smoothing
$\epsilon > 0$, but it concerns regularized fields only. We still have to pass
to the unregularized solution.

\LeanMarker{L:uniform-series-tail}
\begin{lemma}[Uniform series tails from scaled moments]\label{L:uniform-series-tail}
  Fix $(T,x)$. For each positive regularization pair $(\epsilon,\delta)$, let
  $Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}$ be the terms of Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick} for a
  field with nonnegative covariance. Write $u^{\epsilon,\delta}(r) =
  \sum_{n\ge0}r^nY_n^{\epsilon,\delta}$. Suppose that, for some $r > 1$ and
  finite $B_r$,
  \[
    \sup_{\epsilon,\delta>0}
    \E|u^{\epsilon,\delta}(r)|^2\le B_r,
    \qquad
    Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}\longrightarrow X_n
    \text{ in }L^2\text{ for each fixed }n.
  \]
  Then $u = \sum_{n\ge0}X_n$ converges absolutely in $L^2$,
  $u^{\epsilon,\delta}(1) \to u$ in $L^2$, and
  \begin{equation}\label{E:uniform-series-tail}
    \|Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}\|_2,\ \|X_n\|_2
    \le B_r^{1/2}r^{-n},\qquad
    \left\|\sum_{n>N}Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}\right\|_2,
    \left\|\sum_{n>N}X_n\right\|_2
    \le\frac{B_r^{1/2}r^{-(N+1)}}{1-r^{-1}}.
  \end{equation}
  If also $\E|u^{\epsilon,\delta}(1)|^2 \le B_1$ uniformly, then $\E|u|^2 \le
  B_1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick} gives absolute $L^2$ convergence of every
  regularized series and nonnegative mixed moments. Its scaled second moment
  therefore contains the nonnegative term $r^{2n}\E|Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}|^2$,
  so $r^{2n}\E|Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}|^2 \le B_r$. Taking the fixed-order $L^2$
  limit proves the same bound for $X_n$. The geometric series and the triangle
  inequality give all estimates in~\eqref{E:uniform-series-tail}, including
  absolute convergence.

  For each $N$, write both full series as their first $N+1$ terms and their
  tails. Their distance is at most
  \[
    \frac{2B_r^{1/2}r^{-(N+1)}}{1-r^{-1}}
    +\left\|\sum_{n=0}^N(Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}-X_n)\right\|_2.
  \]
  First let $\epsilon,\delta\downarrow0$ at fixed $N$, and then let $N \to
  \infty$. This proves convergence of the full regularized solution; it was not
  assumed. Continuity of the $L^2$ norm passes the bound $B_1$ to $u$. This
  proves Lemma~\ref{L:uniform-series-tail}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Temporal comparison and the full upper bound}

The use of finitely many shifted dyadic grids is classical. For the covering
principle and its antecedents, see
Lerner~\cite{lerner:source2013:simple}*{Section~2.2, p.~3162}.
We give the construction, nesting check and covariance comparison needed for
the time intervals below.

\begin{theorem}[Full upper scale]\label{T:full-upper}
  Under Hypothesis~\ref{H:homog}, let $u$ be the canonical expansion
  solution~\eqref{E:expansion}, constructed as a Stratonovich mild solution in
  Proposition~\ref{P:mild-construction}. Put
  \[
    R_\alpha=\frac{\alpha\pi\mu(B_1)}{2\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}.
  \]
  For every admissible $d = 1,2,3$, there is a finite $C = C(\alpha,\alpha_0)$
  such that
  \begin{equation}\label{E:full-upper-bound}
    \E u^2(T,x)
    \le e(T+2)^d
    \exp\{C R_\alpha^{1/(3-\alpha)}
    T^{(4-\alpha-\alpha_0)/(3-\alpha)}\},\qquad T\ge1.
  \end{equation}
  In particular, this proves Theorem~\ref{T:intro-upper}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  We dominate the temporal covariance by finite-depth shifted interval models
  with a controlled length inventory. We transfer their phase bound to
  constant data on a fixed observation ball, compare the positive terminal
  Wick integrals, and remove noise regularization using a separate uniform
  series-tail bound. All identifications precede the large-time conclusion.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We construct the dyadic temporal majorant and its
  length inventory.
  Zero noise is trivial. Choose $H_0$ with $6T \le H_0 < 12T$. At lengths $h_j =
  H_0 2^{-j}$, use the three grids with endpoints $h_j(n+(-1)^j t)$, $n \in
  \mathbb{Z}$, $t = 0,1/3,2/3$. They are nested: the difference between the
  parent and child shifts in child units is an integer, since $3t \in
  \mathbb{Z}$. For $s \ne r$ in $[0,T]$, choose a scale $3|s-r| < h_j \le
  6|s-r|$. Boundaries of the three grids at this scale have mutual spacing
  $h_j/3 > |s-r|$. Hence, at least one grid interval contains both points,
  including their endpoints after choosing a boundary-free grid. It follows that
  \begin{equation}\label{E:dyadic-time-majorant}
    |s-r|^{-\alpha_0}
    \le6^{\alpha_0}\sum_{\text{three grids},\,|I|\le H_0}
    |I|^{-\alpha_0}\mathbf1_I(s)\mathbf1_I(r)=:K_\infty(s,r).
  \end{equation}
  For context, the sum is at most $C_{\alpha_0}|s-r|^{-\alpha_0}$ off the
  diagonal: only lengths at least $|s-r|$ contribute, with at most one interval
  per grid and length. Endpoint values do not affect the time integrals.

  Keep intervals meeting $[0,T]$ through depth $J$, and use independent static
  smoothed fields with $\sigma_I^2 = 6^{\alpha_0}|I|^{-\alpha_0}$. Their time
  covariance is the corresponding partial sum $K_J$. The decreasing interval
  lengths obey $h_i \le 78T/i$. Indeed, at length $h_j$ there are at most
  $3(T/h_j+2)$ intervals. Summing levels $0,\ldots,j$ bounds their cumulative
  count by
  \[
    6T/h_j+6(j+1)\le6T/h_j+6\,2^j\le78T/h_j,
  \]
  since $H_0 < 12T$ and $2^j = H_0/h_j$. Each index at level $j$ is at most that
  cumulative count, which gives the asserted decreasing-length bound. Thus, one
  may take $L = 78$ and $c = 6^{\alpha_0}$ in
  Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients}.
  Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients} applies uniformly in $J,\epsilon$.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We localize constant initial data on the full
  backward cone.
  To pass from phase data to constant initial data, choose $f_T \in C_c^\infty$
  with $0 \le f_T \le 1$, equal to one on $B_{T+1}$ and supported in $B_{T+2}$,
  and set the velocity to zero. For $|y|+s < T+1$, $G_s*f_T(y) = s$, so
  $C_sf_T(y) = 1$. The support of $G_t$ is contained in $B_t$. Along a chain
  ending at $x \in B_1$ at time $T$, each earlier point $(s,y)$ satisfies $|y|+s
  \le |x|+T < T+1$. Thus, its free initial-data factor is one. Induction on
  Duhamel order identifies the position generated from $(f_T,0)$ at time $T$ on
  $B_1$ with the constant-data model $u_{J,\epsilon}$. This identity can be
  taken in Gaussian $L^2$ term by term. At fixed $J,\epsilon$, each finite-order
  terminal integral is continuous in $x$ as an $L^2(\Omega)$ vector: write its
  Green variables as displacements from $x$, and use continuity of
  $\Gamma_\epsilon$ in its mixed Wick moments. Bounded covariance products and
  the finite terminal Green mass permit dominated convergence. These measurable
  representatives identify the $L^2(\Omega;L^2_x)$ position on $B_1$ with the
  same pointwise constant-data terms. The summable bound below passes this
  identity to their series. At finite $J,\epsilon$, pointwise convergence for
  constant data follows from the bound
  $\sqrt{(2n-1)!!}\,v_{\max}^{n/2}T^{2n}/(2n)!$ on the $n$th position term. The
  model is spatially stationary. Consequently,
  \[
    |B_1|\E|u_{J,\epsilon}(T,0)|^2
    \le\E\|u_{f_T,J,\epsilon}(T)\|_2^2
    \le e\,|B_{T+2}|\exp\{C R_\alpha^{1/\nu}T^{\beta/\nu}\}.
  \]


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We identify the finite interval-model Wick sums
  before comparison.
  We now identify the interval-model Wick sums before comparing them. Its
  centered Gaussian noise has covariance
  \[
    \E[W_{J,\epsilon}(s,y)W_{J,\epsilon}(r,z)]
    =\sum_{I\in\mathcal{F}_J}\sigma_I^2
    \mathbf1_I(s)\mathbf1_I(r)\Gamma_\epsilon(y-z)
    =K_J(s,r)\Gamma_\epsilon(y-z).
  \]
  Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick} applies: at fixed $J,\epsilon$ the field has
  bounded variance, is mean-square continuous in space and is piecewise
  continuous in time. Its exact terminal kernels are
  \eqref{E:terminal-chain-measure}, and every term
  of~\eqref{E:terminal-pairing-integral} has covariance factor
  $K_J(s_j,s_l)\Gamma_\epsilon(y_j-y_l)$, with coefficient one. This comparison
  is made before any time reversal; $K_J$ need not be stationary in time. The
  position kernels and constant initial data are nonnegative. Thus, every such
  integral, including self-pairings and unequal chain lengths, is nonnegative.
  The pointwise $L^2$ convergence proved above identifies the full moment with
  their nonnegative sum. In particular, the moment of every finite partial-order
  sum is at most the full moment, even though samplewise partial sums need not
  increase.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 4.~}We compare the source model through positive
  terminal diagrams.
  From the current author version
  \cite{chen:author2026:time-dependency}*{(2.23)--(2.24), p.~13}, the common
  noise regularization has covariance
  $\gamma_\delta^0(s-r)\Gamma_\epsilon(y-z)$, where
  \[
    0\le\gamma_\delta^0(t)
    =\Gamma(\alpha_0)^{-1}\int_0^{\delta^{-1}}
    e^{-q|t|}q^{\alpha_0-1}\ud q
    \le |t|^{-\alpha_0}.
  \]
  Here $\Gamma(\alpha_0)$ is the Euler Gamma function. At fixed positive
  $\epsilon,\delta$, first take the source's Green-width limit. The bounded
  continuous covariances, weak convergence of the positive Green measures and
  the dominated finite Wick calculation in Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick} identify
  its classical measure-kernel Duhamel terms. For the source's continuous field
  modification, these Bochner terms equal the pathwise smoothed integrals:
  writing $\Phi_n$ for the product of field values, the Gaussian bound in that
  lemma gives $\int\|\Phi_n\|_2\ud\mathsf{g}_n^{T,x} < \infty$. Tonelli gives
  pathwise integrability and Minkowski puts the integral in $L^2$. Fubini
  against every $L^2$ test variable and Bochner duality identify the two
  versions. The same argument applies before the Green-width limit in dimension
  three.

  Write $U_N^{\epsilon,\delta}$ for the sum of source terms of orders
  $0,\ldots,N$ after that Green limit. Write $V_{N,J}^\epsilon$ for the same
  partial-order sum of the finite interval model. Both moments are finite sums
  over exactly the same terminal-chain kernels and pairings; only the covariance
  factors differ. By~\eqref{E:dyadic-time-majorant} each source covariance
  factor is bounded by its $K_\infty$ counterpart. Since $K_J\uparrow K_\infty$,
  nonnegative Tonelli at fixed $N$ yields
  \begin{equation}\label{E:interval-source-comparison}
    \E|U_N^{\epsilon,\delta}(T,x)|^2
    \le\lim_{J\to\infty}\E|V_{N,J}^\epsilon(T,x)|^2
    \le\sup_J\E|u_{J,\epsilon}(T,x)|^2\le B(T),
  \end{equation}
  where $B(T)$ is the right side of~\eqref{E:full-upper-bound}. The middle
  inequality uses the positive pairing sum just identified. No Gaussian field of
  covariance $K_\infty\Gamma_\epsilon$ is constructed; $K_\infty$ is used only
  inside these monotone limits of positive integrals. This is entrywise
  comparison of Wick integrals, not covariance order for arbitrary initial
  phases or a coupling of random solutions.

  The comparison is made at fixed partial order, so each side contains
  finitely many Wick products. The increasing interval depth is then handled
  by monotone convergence of those positive integrals. This does not require
  an infinite-depth Gaussian field, nor a samplewise comparison between the
  two solutions.

  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 5.~}We obtain a scaled source bound that controls all
  order tails.
  The same finite-model argument works after multiplying the source noise by any
  real amplitude $a > 0$. This replaces $\Gamma$ by $a^2\Gamma$ and $R_\alpha$
  by $a^2R_\alpha$, without changing its homogeneity degree or any
  parameter-only constant. Thus,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:regularized-scaled-moment}
    \E|u^{\epsilon,\delta}(a;T,x)|^2
    \le B(T;a):=e(T+2)^d
    \exp\{C(a^2R_\alpha)^{1/\nu}T^\kappa\}.
  \end{equation}
  Here the full regularized series exists by Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick}; it is
  the ordinary Duhamel series with its order-$n$ term multiplied by $a^n$.
  Taking the finite partial-order cutoff to infinity
  in~\eqref{E:interval-source-comparison} is justified at positive
  regularization by that lemma, not by a theorem about the original unsmoothed
  solution.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 6.~}We identify fixed orders and remove the full
  source regularization.
  Lemma~\ref{L:joint-fixed-order} supplies the fixed-order construction: each
  $Y_n^{\epsilon,\delta}$ converges in $L^2$ to $X_n = S_n(g_n)$ as
  $\epsilon,\delta\downarrow0$, with arbitrary relative rates. The bounded-noise
  Green limit and ordered-kernel convention needed to match these terms are
  already verified in Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick}.

  Apply Lemma~\ref{L:uniform-series-tail} with $r = 2$ and $B_r = B(T;2)$. It
  proves absolute $L^2$ convergence of the series defining $u$
  in~\eqref{E:expansion} and convergence of the full regularized solution to
  that series. Taking the limit in~\eqref{E:regularized-scaled-moment} at $a =
  1$ proves the required bound. This series-limit argument is independent of
  Proposition~\ref{P:mixed}, Lemma~\ref{L:crude-corrected}, and the source's
  Section~5 convergence proof. No first-moment asymptotic or printed
  normalization is used. The fixed-order construction just used is proved here
  from the universal pairing bound and the homogeneous norm estimates; neither
  the source's Theorem~4.1 nor its Lemma~4.4 is imported.
  Proposition~\ref{P:mild-construction} separately proves that this same series
  satisfies the source's Definition~2.1: its onefold Stratonovich integral
  equals $u-1$. That construction uses the corrected mixed-vertex crude bound
  for its product and order limits. This is a separate dependency from the
  sharp-scale moment estimate just proved; the source's Section~5(ii) proof is
  not imported. The crude constants used for this identification may depend on
  the fixed covariance; they do not enter~\eqref{E:full-upper-bound}. No
  uniqueness assertion is made.

  The estimate at amplitude two supplies a geometric majorant for the tails,
  while the final bound is passed from amplitude one. The larger auxiliary
  bound is used only to justify convergence of the full series; it is not
  substituted for the final estimate. The covariance amplitude in that final
  estimate remains $R_\alpha^{1/(3-\alpha)}$.

  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 7.~}We record the distinct cutoff limits and the
  parameter-only constant.
  \emph{Cutoff dependence and the order of limits.} The preceding argument uses
  the following distinct statements.
  \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item At fixed total order $2k$, the coefficient estimates are uniform
      in the Gaussian-mode and spatial-band cutoffs, in the finite interval
      family, and in positive spatial smoothing. The local Hermite cutoff $N_i =
      2k_i$ preserves each selected coefficient exactly; it is an algebraic
      choice, not a degree limit. Lemma~\ref{L:fixed-split} and the
      interval-length bound contain no smoothing-dependent variance.
    \item At fixed finite interval family and $\epsilon > 0$, mode and band
      limits identify these coefficients with the Duhamel words. The
      entire-series majorant in Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients} may
      depend on this family and $\epsilon$, through $v_{\max}$. It is used for
      identification, not as a uniform moment estimate. The uniform coefficient
      bound passes to these limits. Localization and positive covariance
      comparison are applied only after removing these cutoffs; positivity of a
      finite-mode covariance is not assumed.
    \item For the resulting finite-depth interval model, the moment bound
      is uniform in $J,\epsilon$. Fix positive source widths $\epsilon,\delta$
      and finite partial order $N$. The Green-width limit is taken first. The
      subsequent $J \to \infty$ comparison
      in~\eqref{E:interval-source-comparison} is monotone convergence of finite
      positive Wick integrals. At these fixed noise widths, $N \to \infty$ is
      justified by Lemma~\ref{L:terminal-wick}, yielding
      \eqref{E:regularized-scaled-moment}. There is no infinite-depth Gaussian
      field or simultaneous mode-depth limit in this step.
    \item At each fixed $T,x,\Gamma$ and order $n$,
      Lemma~\ref{L:joint-fixed-order} gives qualitative $L^2$ convergence as
      $\epsilon,\delta\downarrow0$, with arbitrary relative rates. The bound
      $B(T;2)$ gives tails uniform in these two widths.
      Lemma~\ref{L:uniform-series-tail} first uses their limit at fixed partial
      order, then sends that order to infinity. The final moment bound comes
      from $B(T;1)$, not the larger tail bound $B(T;2)$.
    \item All identifications above are for fixed $T,x,\Gamma$; no
      convergence rate uniform in $T$ or over covariance shapes is claimed. They
      prove~\eqref{E:full-upper-bound} for every fixed $T \ge 1$ with the same
      parameter-only constant. Only after this estimate is proved is $T \to
      \infty$ used for the growth-rate conclusion.
  \end{enumerate}
  The constants come from $L = 78$, $c = 6^{\alpha_0}$ and the parameter-only
  constants of Proposition~\ref{P:interval-coefficients}; covariance enters only
  through $R_\alpha$. The volume ratio is $|B_{T+2}|/|B_1| = (T+2)^d$. This
  proves Theorem~\ref{T:full-upper}.
\end{proof}

{"source_path": "notes/notes_stratonovich_ham_second_moment.tex", "source_revision": "e6fffb9e17f5f534703a6d6ff5e82e3617ec192e", "source_lf": [4973, 5292], "role": "ASSIGNED NEW scientific target block. \section{Finite-order checks in one space dimension}\label{S:G1}

We now compute the total-order-two and total-order-four coefficients in
Proposition~\ref{P:mixed}, first for spatial white noise and then for $\gamma(x)
= c_\alpha|x|^{-\alpha}$ with $0 < \alpha < 1$ and $c_\alpha > 0$. Here $d = 1$
throughout. A total order is the sum of the two Stratonovich orders; it is not
an orthogonal Wiener-chaos order. Write
\[
  A=\{(1,2),(3,4)\},\quad B=\{(1,3),(2,4)\},\quad
  C=\{(1,4),(2,3)\}.
\]
These are the three pairings of four vertices. All finite integrals below retain
the normalization $G(s,y) = \frac12\mathbf1_{\{|y|<s\}}$ and covariance
$|s-r|^{-\alpha_0}\gamma(y-z)$.

\subsection{Spatial white noise: all order-four diagrams}

For $a,b,c \ge 0$, define
\begin{equation}\label{E:interval-area}
  F(a,b,c)=4ab-(a+b-c)_+^2+(|a-b|-c)_+^2.
\end{equation}
This is the area of $\{(x,y):|x|<a,\ |y|<b,\ |x-y|<c\}$. In particular, $F$ is
nonnegative and symmetric in its three arguments. To verify the formula,
subtract from the rectangle the two regions where $|x-y| \ge c$; when $c \ge
|a-b|$ these are two right triangles, and when $c < |a-b|$ the retained strip
has area $4\min(a,b)c$. The three formulas, including $c \ge a+b$,
give~\eqref{E:interval-area}. Symmetry also follows from swapping $x,y$ and the
determinant-one change $(x,y)\mapsto(x,x-y)$.

In the following table, $V$ is a spatial factor and $q_1,q_2$ are nonnegative
time gaps. The contribution of a row is the integral of $V(q_1q_2)^{-\alpha_0}$
over its domain, with ordinary Lebesgue measure in its four listed variables.
The domains are
\[
  \begin{array}{ll}
    (0,4):&a,b,c,d>0,\quad a+b+c+d<t,\\
    (1,3):&0<r<t,\quad a,b,c>0,\quad a+b+c<t,\\
    (2,2):&a,b,c,d>0,\quad a+b<t,\quad c+d<t.
  \end{array}
\]
The letters $a,b,c,d$ denote increments within the nonempty chains; for $(1,3)$,
$r$ is the time of the single vertex. Values on zero-gap sets do not affect
these nonnegative integrals.

\LeanMarker{P:white-four}
\begin{proposition}[White-space order four]\label{P:white-four}
  Suppose that $d = \alpha = 1$ and $\gamma = \delta_0$. Each $J_{m,n,D}(t)$
  with $m+n = 4$ is given by the following table or by exchanging the two
  chains:
  \begin{equation}\label{E:white-four-table}
    \begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c}
      (m,n)&D&V&q_1&q_2\\ \hline
      (0,4)&A&ac/4&b&d\\
      (0,4)&B&a\min(b,c,d)/4&b+c&c+d\\
      (0,4)&C&a\min(b,d)/4&b+c+d&c\\ \hline
      (1,3)&A&\min(r,a)b/4&|r-a|&c\\
      (1,3)&B&F(r,a,\min(b,c))/16&|r-a-b|&b+c\\
      (1,3)&C&F(r,a,c)/16&|r-a-b-c|&b\\ \hline
      (2,2)&A&ac/4&b&d\\
      (2,2)&B&\min(a,c)\min(b,d)/4&|a-c|&|a+b-c-d|\\
      (2,2)&C&F(a,c,\min(b,d))/16&|a-c-d|&|a+b-c|
    \end{array}
  \end{equation}
  The total-order-four contribution is
  \begin{equation}\label{E:total-four}
    2\sum_{D\in\{A,B,C\}}J_{0,4,D}(t)
    +2\sum_{D\in\{A,B,C\}}J_{1,3,D}(t)
    +\sum_{D\in\{A,B,C\}}J_{2,2,D}(t).
  \end{equation}
  Each entry scales as $t^{6-2\alpha_0}$ times its unit-time value.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  We identify the two free spatial positions for each pairing and compute the
  allowed area. We then exchange the chains and collect the time and space
  scaling factors.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We compute the spatial factors for each chain
  split.
  In each pairing identify its first pair's spatial positions with $x$ and the
  other pair's positions with $y$. The two delta covariances have unit mass, so
  spatial integration leaves $1/16$ times the area allowed by the four Green
  indicators. For a single chain the position lists for $A,B,C$ are,
  respectively, $(x,x,y,y)$, $(x,y,x,y)$ and $(x,y,y,x)$. Their areas are $4ac$,
  $4a\min(b,c,d)$ and $4a\min(b,d)$.

  For $(1,3)$ take the single position to be $x$. The long chain has positions
  $(x,y,y)$, $(y,x,y)$ or $(y,y,x)$ for $A,B,C$. The first area is
  $4\min(r,a)b$; the other two areas are $F(r,a,\min(b,c))$ and $F(r,a,c)$. For
  $(2,2)$ the position lists of the two chains are $(x,x),(y,y)$ for $A$,
  $(x,y),(x,y)$ for $B$, and $(x,y),(y,x)$ for $C$. The areas are $4ac$,
  $4\min(a,c)\min(b,d)$ and $F(a,c,\min(b,d))$. In every case reading the paired
  times gives the two gaps in~\eqref{E:white-four-table}. This proves each row,
  with no unlisted pairing or multiplicity. The distributional delta
  integrations can equivalently be obtained by convolution with the source's
  spatial approximate identity: for positive increments the remaining bounded
  interval indicators have only boundary discontinuities, and those boundaries
  have two-dimensional measure zero. The resulting areas converge to the
  displayed areas. They are uniformly bounded at unit time: each smoothed delta
  has mass one, so its integral over its paired coordinates in $[-1,1]^2$ is at
  most two. The four Green factors give a spatial bound $2^2/16 = 1/4$.
  Integrability of the time weights follows from the disjoint-pair bound used in
  Lemma~\ref{L:static-four} below.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We collect the multiplicities and the scaling.
  Exchanging chains gives the $(4,0)$ and $(3,1)$ sums without changing the
  covariance factors. This proves~\eqref{E:total-four}. Finally, scaling all
  four time variables by $t$ gives $t^4$ from their measure, $t^2$ from the area
  and $t^{-2\alpha_0}$ from the covariance factors. This proves
  Proposition~\ref{P:white-four}.
\end{proof}

\input{notes/figures/white-strip}

\begin{lemma}[Static finite-order calibration]\label{L:static-four}
  As $\alpha_0\downarrow0$, each unit-time coefficient in
  Proposition~\ref{P:white-four} converges to the entry below:
  \begin{equation}\label{E:static-four-table}
    \begin{array}{c|ccc|c}
      (m,n)&A&B&C&\text{sum}\\ \hline
      (0,4)&1/2880&1/8640&1/5760&11/17280\\
      (1,3)&1/576&29/34560&1/640&143/34560\\
      (2,2)&1/144&1/480&31/17280&187/17280
    \end{array}
  \end{equation}
  Thus, the static total-order-four contribution is $11t^6/540$. The static
  total-order-two contribution is $t^3/3$. These are coefficients, not
  large-time asymptotic constants.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We first justify the static limit by an integrable time-cube bound. We then
  integrate time slacks at fixed spatial positions and evaluate the remaining
  polynomial sector integrals.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We pass to the static coefficient by dominated
  convergence.
  On the unit-time domain all original time coordinates belong to $[0,1]$. The
  spatial factor is bounded by $1/4$, since all positions lie in $[-1,1]$ and
  the two free positions have area at most four. Fix $0 < \bar{a} < 1$. For $0
  \le \alpha_0 \le \bar{a}$, the temporal factors are bounded by the product
  with exponent $\bar{a}$. On the full time cube its integral is
  \[
    \left(\int_0^1\int_0^1|s-r|^{-\bar{a}}\ud s\ud r\right)^2
    =\left(\frac{2}{(1-\bar{a})(2-\bar{a})}\right)^2<\infty,
  \]
  because the two pairs use disjoint labels. Dominated convergence permits
  setting $\alpha_0 = 0$ in every row.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We reduce the coefficients to spatial sectors.
  We evaluate the resulting integrals by an independent spatial calculation. For
  fixed free positions $x,y$, let $L_1,L_2$ be the sums of absolute spatial
  increments along the two chains, starting at zero. Integrating their time
  increments first gives
  \begin{equation}\label{E:static-sector}
    J_{m,n,D}(1)\big|_{\alpha_0=0}
    =\frac{1}{16m!n!}\int_{\R^2}
    (1-L_1(x,y))_+^m(1-L_2(x,y))_+^n\ud x\ud y.
  \end{equation}
  An empty-chain factor in this formula is defined to be one. Indeed, writing
  each time increment as its required absolute spatial increment plus a
  nonnegative slack leaves a simplex of volume $(1-L_\rho)_+^{n_\rho}/n_\rho!$.
  Put $w = |x-y|$. The length pairs are
  \[
    \begin{array}{c|ccc}
      (m,n)&A&B&C\\ \hline
      (0,4)&(0,|x|+w)&(0,|x|+3w)&(0,|x|+2w)\\
      (1,3)&(|x|,|x|+w)&(|x|,|y|+2w)&(|x|,|y|+w)\\
      (2,2)&(|x|,|y|)&(|x|+w,|x|+w)&(|x|+w,|y|+w)
    \end{array}
  \]

  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We evaluate and sum the sector contributions.
  Divide the plane into $0 \le x \le y$, $0 \le y \le x$, $x \ge 0 \ge y$ and
  their reflected sectors. Use $(x,y) = (u,u+v),(u+v,u),(u,-v)$, respectively,
  where $u,v \ge 0$. Each map has determinant of absolute value one. In these
  three sectors the integrals of $(1-L_1)_+^m(1-L_2)_+^n$, before multiplication
  by $2/(16m!n!)$, are
  \begin{equation}\label{E:sector-values}
    \begin{array}{c|c|ccc}
      (m,n)&D&0\le x\le y&0\le y\le x&x\ge0\ge y\\ \hline
      (0,4)&A&1/30&1/60&1/60\\
      (0,4)&B&1/90&1/120&1/360\\
      (0,4)&C&1/60&1/90&1/180\\
      (1,3)&A&1/24&3/160&11/480\\
      (1,3)&B&1/72&3/160&11/1440\\
      (1,3)&C&1/48&1/30&1/48\\
      (2,2)&A&1/18&1/18&1/9\\
      (2,2)&B&1/30&1/60&1/60\\
      (2,2)&C&31/1440&31/1440&31/2160
    \end{array}
  \end{equation}
  All entries follow by integrating polynomials over the two linear constraints
  $L_1 \le 1$, $L_2 \le 1$. For example, $\int_{u,v\ge0}(1-pu-qv)_+^4\ud u\ud v
  = 1/(30pq)$. The last row's first entry and last entry are, explicitly,
  \[
    \begin{split}
      &\int_0^{1/2}\ud v\int_0^{1-2v}
      (1-u-v)^2(1-u-2v)^2\ud u=\frac{31}{1440},\\
      &\int_0^{1/3}\ud u\int_0^{(1-u)/2}
      (1-2u-v)^2(1-u-2v)^2\ud v\\
      &\quad+\int_{1/3}^{1/2}\ud u\int_0^{1-2u}
      (1-2u-v)^2(1-u-2v)^2\ud v=\frac{31}{2160}.
    \end{split}
  \]
  The stated lengths and linear bounds similarly specify every entry
  of~\eqref{E:sector-values}. Summing and multiplying by $2/(16m!n!)$
  proves~\eqref{E:static-four-table}. Applying the multiplicities
  in~\eqref{E:total-four} gives $11/540$. Lemma~\ref{L:white-test} at $\alpha_0
  = 0$ gives the order-two value. This proves Lemma~\ref{L:static-four}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Riesz covariance: order two and an order-four integral}

\LeanMarker{P:riesz-small}
\begin{proposition}[Riesz finite coefficients]\label{P:riesz-small}
  Suppose $d = 1$, $0 < \alpha < 1$, $0 < \alpha_0 < 1$, and $\gamma(y) =
  c_\alpha|y|^{-\alpha}$ with $c_\alpha > 0$. With $\beta = 4-\alpha-\alpha_0$,
  the two order-two terms are
  \begin{equation}\label{E:riesz-two}
    \begin{split}
      \E X_2(t,x)
      &=\frac{c_\alpha t^\beta}
      {(1-\alpha)(2-\alpha-\alpha_0)(3-\alpha-\alpha_0)
      (4-\alpha-\alpha_0)},\\
      \E X_1(t,x)^2
      &=\frac{c_\alpha}{(1-\alpha)(2-\alpha)}
      \int_0^t v^{-\alpha_0}
      \left[\frac{(2t-v)^{3-\alpha}-v^{3-\alpha}}{2(3-\alpha)}
      -(t-v)v^{2-\alpha}\right]\ud v.
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  Their total is $\E X_1^2+2\E X_2$.

  The following explicit unit-domain integral gives every order-four term. For
  $m+n = 4$, let
  \[
    H_m=\{h\in(0,\infty)^4:\ \sum_{l=1}^m h_l<1,
    \ \sum_{l=m+1}^4 h_l<1\},
  \]
  with an empty sum equal to zero. For each index $l$, let $b(l)$ be the first
  index in its own nonempty chain, and define
  \[
    S_l(h)=\sum_{i=b(l)}^l h_i,\qquad
    Y_l(h,U)=\sum_{i=b(l)}^l h_iU_i,
  \]
  where $U_1,\ldots,U_4$ are independent uniform variables on $[-1,1]$. Then,
  for $D = A,B,C$,
  \begin{equation}\label{E:riesz-four}
    \begin{split}
      J_{m,n,D}(t)
      ={}&c_\alpha^2t^{2\beta}
      \int_{H_m}\left(\prod_{l=1}^4h_l\right)
      \prod_{(j,q)\in D}|S_j(h)-S_q(h)|^{-\alpha_0}\\
      &\hspace{8mm}\times
      \E_U\left[\prod_{(j,q)\in D}
      |Y_j(h,U)-Y_q(h,U)|^{-\alpha}\right]\ud h.
    \end{split}
  \end{equation}
  This is a finite nonnegative integral. It specifies all five splits and all
  three pairings without an unspecified kernel. The total coefficient uses the
  multiplicities in~\eqref{E:total-four}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  We compute the two order-two coefficients directly. For order four, we
  express Green increments through uniform variables and check finiteness
  before using the resulting unit-domain formula.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 1.~}We evaluate the order-two terms.
  Direct integration on the two intervals gives
  \[
    \int_{\R^2}G(s,y)G(r,z)c_\alpha|y-z|^{-\alpha}\ud y\ud z
    =\frac{c_\alpha\big((s+r)^{2-\alpha}-|s-r|^{2-\alpha}\big)}
    {2(1-\alpha)(2-\alpha)}.
  \]
  Integrate on $s > r$ and double, set $v = s-r$, and integrate $r$ over
  $(0,t-v)$. This proves the second formula in~\eqref{E:riesz-two}. In the
  single-chain term with increments $r,v$, the spatial integral is $c_\alpha
  rv^{1-\alpha}/(1-\alpha)$. Thus, the remaining integral is
  $c_\alpha(1-\alpha)^{-1}\int_{r+v<t}rv^{1-\alpha-\alpha_0}\ud r\ud v$, which
  gives its first formula by two elementary integrations.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 2.~}We identify the four-increment formula and its
  scaling.
  For order four set each spatial increment equal to $h_lU_l$. The Green factor
  $\frac12\mathbf1_{[-h_l,h_l]}$ is $h_l$ times the law of $h_lU_l$. The map
  from increments to vertex positions is triangular within each chain with
  determinant one. Consequently, the four spatial integrals give exactly the
  product $\prod_lh_l$ and the expectation in~\eqref{E:riesz-four}; the factors
  $1/16$ are already incorporated in the four uniform probability laws. The time
  increments give the domain $H_m$ and the differences $S_j-S_q$. Scaling time
  and space by $t$ contributes $t^8$ from the eight integrations and
  $t^{-2\alpha-2\alpha_0}$ from the two covariance factors, giving $t^{2\beta}$.


  \medskip
  \noindent\textit{Step 3.~}We check integrability on the enlarged time and
  space cubes.
  Finiteness can be checked without a numerical argument. On the unit-time
  domain all vertex positions lie in $[-1,1]$. Enlarge the time domain to
  $[0,1]^4$ and the spatial domain to $[-1,1]^4$, and bound the Green indicators
  by one. The two time covariance factors involve disjoint pairs and are
  integrable since $\alpha_0 < 1$. The same is true of the two spatial factors
  since $\alpha < 1$. Tonelli's theorem now gives a finite bound. This proves
  Proposition~\ref{P:riesz-small}.
\end{proof}

{"source_path": "notes/notes_stratonovich_ham_second_moment.tex", "source_revision": "cdde711c248862f33207860107dd0e6b31d60852", "source_lf": [367, 409], "role": "UNASSIGNED exact model/covariance/data/zero case.", "bytes": 1986, "sha256": "54b3654b8aa19a0dbc1d792e95f7286c0924fbf20d8c6da771a5835aac7655b7"}
